--- Page 1 ---
Machine learning for 
cognitive modelling:
principles & applications
Dr. Ben Harvey
Experimental Psychology
b.m.harvey@uu.nl
Why study machine learning?
• A very powerful data analysis method
• Gives methods to interpret complex data
• Increasingly widely used in scientific 
research
• A broadly applicable method to study data
• Large data sets are increasing       
common due to advances in IT            
(‘big data’)
• Many applications to business              
and technology
Tech applications
Image labelling
Game opponents
Weather prediction
Autonomous vehicles
Recommendation engines
In all of these cases, huge 
amounts of measurements are 
available, either with correct labels 
or known outcomes we are trying 
to predict for future examples.

--- Page 2 ---
What is machine learning?
• The field of science that ‘gives computers the 
ability to learn without being explicitly programmed’ 
(Arthur Samuel, 1959)
So this field has been around for a 
long time
Samuel showed a machine learning 
algorithm many games of checkers 
(draughts) to learn which board 
positions are likely to lead to a 
win. These are again known 
outcomes we are trying to predict 
for future cases. The only 
programmed rule was the way the 
pieces are allowed to move.
What is machine learning?
• The field of science that ‘gives computers the 
ability to learn without being explicitly programmed’ 
(Arthur Samuel, 1959)
• ‘A computer program is said to learn from 
experience E with respect to some class of tasks T 
and performance measure P if its performance at 
tasks in T, as measured by P, improves with 
experience E.’ (Tom Mitchell, 1998)
• Expressed in organisational terms, not cognitive 
terms
But Samuel’s definition of machine 
learning uses the word ‘learn’ 
without any definitions
It also uses ‘computer’, which is a 
synonym of ‘machine’ 
Mitchell provided a more formal 
definition
By describing a fundamental 
operation in terms of inputs and 
outputs, we get a more 
generalisable definition. This 
definition avoids suggesting that 
machine learning and human 
learning should use similar 
approaches or rely on similar 
mechanisms.
Learning to filter spam
• ‘A computer program is said to learn from experience E 
with respect to some class of tasks T and performance 
measure P if its performance at tasks in T, as measured 
by P, improves with experience E.’ (Tom Mitchell, 1998)
• Task: ’Classify which emails are wanted (not spam) vs 
unwanted (spam)’
• Experience: Watching humans labels emails (training 
set)
• Performance: The proportion of new emails (test set) 
classified correctly

--- Page 3 ---
Supervised and unsupervised 
machine learning
• Supervised learning: feed in example inputs and their desired 
outputs (training set).
• Goal is a rule that maps new inputs (test set) to correct outputs
• Can have discrete class outputs (classification task, like a spam 
filter) or continuous outputs (regression task, like rainfall 
prediction)
• Unsupervised learning: No desired outputs are given.
• Goal is to find structure in the inputs (like clustering data into 
groups)
• Reinforcement learning (intermediate): Given output of a complex 
task like driving (did I crash?) or game play (did I win?). 
• Not given information about how close the process is to the goal
Supervised learning is impossible 
where we don’t know (or have) a 
desired output (training data)
Model assessment
• In supervised learning, test the proportion of test set that is 
classified correctly
• ‘Correct’ answer must be know by experimenter
• Test set and training set must be independent (cross-
validation)
• Split or holdout cross-validation method
• Typically 2/3 training and 1/3 test
• Best to re-train the model with different splits to demonstrate 
generalisation
• Leave-one-out cross-validation
• Train model on N-1 example test set
• Test on example N (test set)
• Repeat with (all) different examples left out and used as test
• Correct and incorrect proportions (accuracy), sensitivity & 
specificity (formal discriminability)
Unsupervised learning can’t be 
tested the same way, because the 
‘correct’ answer is unknown
If we want to model a system, 
what do we already know?
• We have a large, complex data set, but do we:
• Know nothing about it 
• Easy to find patterns, difficult to interpret
• Have a specific hypothesis we want to test
• Harder to set up learning model, but easier to 
interpret
• Already know much relevant information
• Should consider this in our learning model
• Likely to constrain model inputs and structure
• More powerful (statistically)

--- Page 4 ---
Relationship to statistics
• Machine learning can capture very complex relationships between input 
states and outputs
• But it is also VERY sensitive to simpler relationships, like: 
• One input variable can be enough to classify output above chance
• A T-test does this, no machine learning needed
• Machine learning (for classification) tells us there is some relationship, a 
T-test tells us what relationship
• One input variable’s magnitude can be sufficient to predict the magnitude of 
an output variable
• Correlations do this (for regression), and also tell us what the relationship 
is. Preferable to machine learning.
• Two input variable states can be enough to predict/classify output
• 2-way ANOVA with interaction terms (classification), bivariate regression 
(regression) or ANCOVA (combined classification & regression)
• Any small number of variables can be enough
• N-way ANOVA, multivariate regression, complex Bayesian modelling and 
other advanced statistics. All reveal what the relationship is.
• Machine learning can do all of these, but often acts as a ‘black box’
So machine learning can be used 
as ‘lazy statistics’. 
It rarely identifies what the 
relationship is between inputs and 
outputs.
But it provides a very flexible 
single method to reveal SOME 
relationship exists.
This can be very useful for 
engineering, where we don’t need 
to know why something works.
But it is very limiting for scientific 
research, where we want to 
understand the system we are 
studying
Statistics are still important to 
determine if the classification 
performance you see is significant. 
This can get pretty complex, so 
rather than avoiding statistics, 
good machine learning relies on 
very advanced statistics
The story so far…
• Machine learning is a widely applicable method to:
• Demonstrate a relationship between two groups of data 
(supervised, e.g. map inputs and expected outputs)
• OR find patterns in complex data sets (unsupervised)
• Should consider what we already know about the system
• Do we already know something that can inform our 
model?
• How much unknown do we need to capture?
• With simpler data sets or fewer unknowns, traditional 
statistics is often a better choice
• If there are a few unknowns to capture, stats will show 
how these are related to the results
• ML typically just shows SOME relationship exists
So, now let’s look at some 
examples of machine learning in 
action to see how these 
advantages and disadvantages 
work in practice.

--- Page 5 ---
Many of the examples I show here 
come from functional MRI of the 
human brain, my own field.
Machine learning and modelling are 
well suited to fMRI data, because it 
takes very large numbers of 
measurements (millions) at a time, 
and repeats these at many points in 
time.
This is pretty big data: an hour of MRI 
data collection is between 2 and 10 
gigabytes.
You may think of fMRI as showing 
which brain area increases its activity 
when we perform a particular task or 
view a particular stimulus.
Here we see the areas that are active 
when we move the right hand. 
Specifically, we see the T-statistic 
magnitude as different colours. This is 
a univariate statistic, showing the sites 
where the brain’s activity changes 
between two states of a sensory input 
or two states of a task. 
So these are areas that give a 
significantly different activity when we 
change between moving and not 
moving the hand (the input to this 
analysis).
This is a large area of the brain, and 
knowing the location of these 
responses doesn’t tell us much about 
what is happening in this area.
Here I will describe fMRI experiments 
that don’t do this, but instead aim to 
discover patterns in the data or to 
reveal mechanisms of human neural 
processing.
These are two very different aims, and 
choosing which to pursue depends on 
what we already know about the 
response we want to characterise.

--- Page 6 ---
Map organisation sensory & 
motor cortices
If we look more closely in this area, 
we can see that its detailed structure 
is far more complex than a big blob 
lighting up. 
We are seeing the activity of the hand 
area in the motor cortex, driving the 
movement, and the hand area of the 
somatosensory cortex responding to 
the resulting sensations. 
Both of these areas are organised as 
maps: the body’s surface is spatially 
mapped onto the brain’s surface. 
So it seems likely we can reveal more 
than just which blob lights up: there is 
detailed spatial structure within that 
blob.
14
Brian Wandell (1999)
Director, Stanford Center for 
Cognitive and Neurobiological 
Imaging
“Prior to fMRI, neuroimaging primarily tested hypotheses 
about the localization of function by asking whether two 
stimuli (or tasks) caused statistically different signals 
within the brain. The SNR (signal quality) of fMRI is large 
enough to measure the detailed characteristics of 
differences, not just their presence or absence.”
Computational neuroimaging
14
Serge Dumoulin (2008)
Director, Spinoza Centre for 
Neuroimaging, Amsterdam
In the Netherlands, this idea was 
pioneered by Brian’s student, 
Serge Dumoulin. 
Mainstream functional MRI no 
longer focusses on univariate 
activation for localisation of 
function (‘which bit lights up’). 
Instead we use variants of machine 
learning to understand the brain’s 
information processing.
Because vision is the best-
understood neural system, most of 
the pioneering work here is done 
in visual processing.
So many of the applications we see 
here focus on vision.
Support vector machines (SVM)
• Can classify based on which 
side of a decision boundary (a 
point, line, plane or hyperplane) 
the new input falls
• Given training set of input 
classes and measured 
responses, builds a model that 
correctly classifies new 
responses into these classes
• Very common supervised 
approach for classification & 
regression
• Widely used in fMRI analysis 
(multi-voxel pattern analysis, 
MVPA)
Measure 1 response
Measure 2 response
Start: So let’s look at some example 
methods to squeeze more information 
out of a complex data set like this.
Like many measurement techniques, 
fMRI takes very many recordings 
simultaneously. 
To explain how we analyse the 
patterns in these recordings, I will 
simplify this to just 2 recordings 
because I can show the concepts on a 
2-dimensional screen. But these 
principles generalise to any number of 
recordings from a brain region of any 
size.
When both responses change in the 
same way, their average response 
gives the same information as the 
pattern: one class gives a high 

--- Page 7 ---
response and one gives a low 
response
In MRI, this is common, because an 
area may generally increase or 
decrease its responses.
In this case, statistics is required, but 
no learning, because the overall 
response is different but the detailed 
PATTERN across the large group of 
measurements has no extra 
information.
BUT when one measurement’s 
response is higher for class A and the 
other measurement’s response is 
higher for class B, their average 
response can be very similar while the 
pattern of responses is very different.
We can separate these two sets of 
responses using a line, so any new 
response pattern measured falls on 
one side of the line and we know it is 
likely to belong to the class on that 
side.
Support vector machines (SVM)
So this makes fMRI able to detect 
much smaller response changes or 
interacting response patterns, 
while regular univariate statistics 
is limited to cases where the mean 
responses are far apart.
But also note that, by looking at 
the pattern across multiple 
recording sites, these are all 
grouped in the pattern analysis.
The responses of individual 
recording sites are not analysed, 
and the structure of the resulting 
boundary is not analyses. We just 
see that there is SOME repeatable 
pattern to the responses.

--- Page 8 ---
Classifiers
Kamitani and Tong (2005), Haynes and Rees (2005; 2006)
So let’s look at a specific example of SVM 
use: decoding image orientation
Using relatively large (3x3x3mm) 
recording sites, we can decode which 
orientation was shown
i.e. we can learn the relationship between 
voxel activity pattern and orientation, then 
determine which was orientation was 
shown by looking at the voxel activity 
pattern.
Here this is done for three early visual 
processing areas, V1, V2 and V3.
This also works for imagined orientation: 
we can determine which orientation a 
subject imagined from their brain activity
This certainly tells us that V1’s responses 
have some information about orientation.
The problem here is that we don’t know 
what information the classifier used: it 
distinguished orientations based on V1’s 
entire activity.
In fact, we already knew that V1’s 
responses have some information about 
orientation.
Neural responses to edge 
orientation
Orientation
selectivity
Hubel & Wiesel (1959)
Because long ago it was shown 
that single neurons show a 
maximum response for a 
particular orientation.

--- Page 9 ---
19
Orientation ‘pinwheels’
~5mm
Preferred orientation (maximum response)
If we look at a small piece of 
cortex, we see different locations 
have different preferred 
orientations (colours). So showing 
different orientations should 
produce different response 
patterns.
Note the scale here: we see a very 
broad range of orientation 
preferences in any 3x3 mm 
square.
Classifiers
Kamitani and Tong (2005), Haynes and Rees (2005; 2006)
The initial explanation proposed 
for this ability to decode 
orientation at a coarse spatial 
scale was that each voxel 
contained a slightly different 
balance of orientation preferences.
This would lead to slight 
differences in the fine-scale voxel 
activation
21
Orientation bias
But we also know some more 
about this system, which 
questions this interpretation.
Neurons in the human visual 
cortex each respond to stimuli at a 
specific place in the visual field 
(retina)
These ‘preferred positions’ change 
gradually across the brain’s 
surface, forming maps of the 
visual field on the brain’s surface.
Because nearby neurons all 
respond to similar positions, a 
larger MRI recording site (voxel) 
also has a preferred position to 
which it responds: this is a change 

--- Page 10 ---
over a much larger scale that is 
easy to see with fMRI.
22
Orientation bias
But if we change orientation while stimulating 
all positions, we see very similar large-scale 
maps of orientation
Each recording site has more orientation 
preferences pointing towards the central visual 
field
So this large-scale bias, just like the fine 
column-level bias, would allow orientations 
decoding
Ten years later, scientists still argue about the 
origin of this decoding ability. 
And this is because the machine learning of the 
pattern only shows there is SOME information in 
the response pattern, not what that information 
is.
Using more computational methods, it is 
becoming clear that both of these sources of 
information contribute.
Also, if a human observer can tell the difference 
between two stimuli, we can be certain that 
there is some location in their brain that holds 
some information about stimulus state. 
So is it really important to show that the brain 
contains some information about orientation, if 
we don’t also show what information that is?  

--- Page 11 ---
Applications: Vegetative state 
(coma)
Owen et al, Science, 2006
Regardless of the source of the 
information, MVPA is widely applicable
Here, we see brain activity when a patient 
and a normal healthy participant are asked 
to imagine playing tennis, compared to 
when imagining exploring a city.
These make clear activations in specific 
places, though the researchers used MVPA 
to classify the patterns as it allows very 
fast classification of each trial, which can 
be done while the participants are still in 
the scanner.
This study is interesting because the 
patient group here was in a prolonged 
vegetative state, a coma in which they 
were assumed to be unaware of their 
surroundings.
This shows that they could understand 
questions and give a binary response 
through their brain activity.
So here the research question is not how 
the brain is processing information. The 
question is whether the brain is 
responding at all, as it had been assumed 
that these patients were not aware of the 
world around them. MVPA gives a simple 
answer.
24
Classifying object categories
• If we show awake humans in fMRI 
scanners categorized visual images, 
we can associate a pattern of brain 
activity with each category
24
Horikawa et al, Science, 2013
Similarly, MVPA decoders are an 
efficient way to associate which 
pattern of brain activity 
corresponds to different types of 
image.
In this study, the experimenters 
showed participants different 
types of labelled image and 
learned the response pattern for 
each type.

--- Page 12 ---
25
• We can then wake them up when 
dreaming and ask what they were 
dreaming about
25
Classifying dream categories
They then let them fall asleep, saw 
when they started dreaming and 
woke them up to ask what they 
were dreaming about.
They used the patterns they had 
learned to analyse their brain 
activity and predict what objects 
they were dreaming about
26
• And we can predict, better than by 
chance, what they will say by examining 
their brain activity while dreaming
• However, this approach looks at object selective 
areas, and cannot reconstruct the images or 
determine what the dream might have looked like
26
Classifying dream categories
MVPA classification with SVMs
• Widely used, simple method to find patterns in large, complex 
fMRI data sets
• After learning patterns associated with different stimuli, algorithm 
can predict which (unknown) stimulus was likely produced an 
observed response
• Demonstrates that the brain are contains ‘information’ about this 
stimulus type (i.e. a repeatable response pattern)
• Even when there is no change in average response amplitude
• (also sensitive to average amplitude changes, regular stats)
• However, no information about WHAT information is present (i.e. 
what was used to achieve the decoding)
• Therefore prone to false positive results and unclear 
interpretation

--- Page 13 ---
MVPA cross-classification
But despite these limitations, 
MVPA can be used to show 
interesting things.
Here cross-classification shows 
that the classifier trained on 
responses at time x can accurately 
classify responses from time x, 
showing that some of the response 
is shared between these two times.
Note here that if training on 
responses at time x and accurately 
decoding responses at time y, the 
responses at time x and y are 
correlated.
So regular statistics can be used 
the same way.
What this adds is that we are not 
just looking at the response to a 
single condition, we are looking at 
the ability to distinguish between 
conditions. So the responses to 
both conditions are being included 
in this model.
Representational similarity 
analysis (RSA)
• An unsupervised regression approach with many 
similarities to SVM’s supervised classification
• SVM determines which side of a decision 
boundary each input falls
• RSA determines how far each stimulus lies 
from each other (no boundary, no decision)
• Better when we have many classes, or no 
clear classes at all
• ‘Distance’ here is difference between response 
amplitudes of all measures
• FMRI voxel activations
• EEG electrode responses
• Individual neuron firing rates
• Human performance or similarity ratings
• Computational model units
• Distances are easy to compute: Pythagoras 
theorem
• or (more simply) dissimilarity = 1 - correlation
Dissimilarity2=(XA-XB)2+(YA-YB)2…(NA-NB)2
Measure 1 response
Measure 2 response
Your lab assignment is based on 
this technique, and implements 
the approaches on the following 
slides.
End: So here we are more explicitly 
looking at the correlation between 
the patterns of activity each input 
produces.

--- Page 14 ---
Representational similarity 
analysis (RSA)
Here we see a little group of fMRI 
voxels (recording sites) that 
changes its pattern of responses 
when shown different objects.
We can summarise how different, 
or dissimilar, these response 
patterns are as one minus the 
correlation coefficient.
This can be repeated for a large 
set of object pairs to give a 
representational dissimilarity 
matrix, summarising all 
differences between response 
patterns for this stimulus set.
This is the really important idea 
here: we can summarise the 
responses to all objects as the 
pattern of similarities of responses 
to ALL object pairs.
Each group of many 
measurements (here voxels) is 
summarised to a single value 
(dissimilarity, 1-correlation). Then 
that is repeated for ALL pairs of 
objects to give a fingerprint of 
which inputs give more similar 
responses and which give less 
similar responses. This fingerprint 
is for each pair of inputs.
This is rather abstracted from the 
measurements taken, as it 
analyses the response differences 
across all voxels and simplifies 
this to give the similarity of 
response patterns per object pair.
The really great thing about RSA is 
we can examine and compare 
patterns of responses measured 
by many different methods.
For example, we could also ask the 
same subjects to arrange objects 
by their perceived similarity. We 
can then measure the distance 
between each pair of objects to 
produce a comparable dissimilarity 
matrix.
If the resulting representational 
dissimilarity matrices for a brain 
area’s activity and perceptual 
judgements are similar, this 
suggests that the brain area’s 

--- Page 15 ---
activity may underlie the 
perception of similarity between 
these objects.
We can also analyse how different 
the pixels are in each pair of 
images, and compare this to the 
dissimilarity matrix to see if this 
brain area’s response is consistent 
with this analysis.
This gets away from simply 
showing that there is some 
information that distinguishes two 
inputs, and allows us to test 
specific hypotheses about what 
underlies the observed response 
RSA and multidimensional 
scaling
RSA dissimilarity matrices can be hard to 
read
Along the diagonal, we see the high 
similarity between repeated responses to 
the same image (blues)
We see (perhaps) four clusters of relatively 
similar images
We can use a complex analysis, 
multidimensional scaling, to project these 
dissimilarity ‘distances’ to two 
perpendicular dimensions
Here, it is easier to see what has clustered 
together and which clusters are more 
similar to others
For example, faces and body parts 
produce more similar response patterns 
than faces and places, so they are closer 
together in the multidimensional scaling 
arrangement on the right.
RSA and hypothesis testing
To test a hypothesis, we can first 
compare the dissimilarity matrix 
predicted by our hypothesis 
against our measured matrix.
So statistics comes in here. We 
have used an unsupervised ML 
method to reduce our highly 
complex data to simpler patterns. 
We must now test hypotheses 
about the structure of those 
patterns. 
Again, ML results should always be 
analysed statistically. So in 
modelling cognitive systems ML is 
not a way to avoid statistics, but a 
way to give your statistics extra 

--- Page 16 ---
possibilities in complex data.
Here we see different ways to split 
the images shown.
We can hypothesise that all 
animate objects are represented 
similarly, and all inanimate objects 
are represented similarly
Or we can hypothesise that all 
faces are represented similarly, 
and all non-faces are represented 
similarly.
Importantly in these dissimilarity 
matrices, we are not characterising 
whether an object is animate or is 
a face. 
We are instead characterising 
whether two objects have the 
SAME STATE of animacy or 
faceness. Two animate objects are 
similar, two inanimate objects are 
ALSO similar, one animate object 
and one inanimate object are 
dissimilar.
A more complex hypothesis
Here we hypothesise that these 
differences in neural 
representation underlie differences 
in perceived similarity
So we correlate behavioural 
judgements of similarity to the 
similarity pattern of neural 
representations
This will show when (or where in 
the brain) neural representations 
are consistent with behavioural 
patterns

--- Page 17 ---
This can be used to show that 
similarity patterns are more closely 
related when they are measured twice 
from the same brain than when 
measured from different brains (top).
This shows that there is pattern of 
similarities that is specific to each 
person.
We also see that similarity patterns 
from fMRI and behavioural measures 
are more closely related if they come 
from the same person rather than 
different people. 
This shows that these brain similarity 
patterns specifically predict each 
person’s perception.
Why RSA?
• Reveals inherent structure in a complex, high-
dimensional data
• i.e. data with many independent measurements
• Determines what is important in determining 
patterns of response
• Such data is common in neuroscience
• Easy to compare neural/behavioural representations 
across very different measurement methods
• No other method can do this
Machine learning: statistics, 
classification, and similarity analyses
• When we have a specific hypothesis about what changes between two 
experimental conditions, traditional statistics is ideal
• Tests for the specific, hypothesised relationship(s)
• Classification with support vector machines is useful for detecting interacting 
differences between many measurements taken in (usually) 2 conditions
• But does not easily show what differed
• This may not matter for our research question
• RSA simplifies complex response patterns to MANY conditions to a fingerprint of 
the pairwise similarities in the response patterns they produce (a dissimilarity 
matrix)
• These fingerprints can be tested against what would be expected for different 
hypotheses
• These fingerprints can be compared between methods, revealing 
relationships between different measurements that cannot otherwise be 
compared
• A data-driven but easily interpretable way to simplify complex cognitive 
neuroscience data
• Applicable to a very broad set of research questions



==================================
FILE: Lectures\4 - Machine learning 2.pdf
==================================

--- Page 1 ---
Machine learning for 
cognitive modelling:
biologically inspired models
Dr. Ben Harvey
Experimental Psychology
b.m.harvey@uu.nl
1
The fMRI experiments I described in the 
last class aimed to discover patterns in 
large, complex fMRI data sets, or to test 
specific hypotheses about patterns in 
these data. 
One problem here is that the resulting 
models don’t show how the brain is 
processing the inputs, only that there is 
information in a particular area or that 
one thing is represented more or less 
similarly to another.
If we want to model a system, 
what do we already know?
• We have a large, complex data set, but do we:
• Know nothing about it 
• Easy to find patterns, difficult to interpret
• Have a specific hypothesis we want to test
• Harder to set up learning model, but easier to 
interpret
• Already know much relevant information
• Should consider this in our learning model
• Likely to constrain model inputs and structure
• More powerful (statistically)
The experiments we look at today aim 
to reveal mechanisms of human neural 
processing, rather than just where we 
see information in the pattern of 
responses.
These are two very different aims, and 
depend on what we already know.
In the case of the fMRI in the visual 
cortex, we know a lot about the system 
we are trying to model, so it is useful to 
constrain our learning model’s structure 
using this information.
Biologically inspired analysis 
methods
• Methods from last class use statistical similarity 
without taking into account the properties of the 
system (brain)
• Other approaches include known properties of the 
system
• Organise the machine learning model following 
these properties
• Learn the parameters of that model
• i.e. how information is encoded in the brain
• Then predict how the brain will respond to a new 
input based on the parameters of that input
At END: One important distinction here 
is that the previous class looked at 
decoding models: can we make a model 
that predicts what responses we expect 
to see in the test set?
The models we look at today are called 
encoding models: they model how the 
brain encodes information. I find this a 
much richer, deeper goal.
To do this, it makes sense to include 
what we already know about how the 
brain encodes information.
An encoding model can also predict the 
responses we would expect to see in the 
test set: an encoding model can be used 
for decoding. However, a decoding 
model CANNOT really show how the 
brain encodes information.

--- Page 2 ---
Today’s Topics
• Visual space encoding models
• Object number encoding models
• Event timing encoding models
• Object identity encoding models
• Deep convolutional neural network 
models
5
Neural responses to edge orientation
You may remember these 
orientation-selective cells from 
the last class. 
These respond to edges with a 
particular position and 
orientation, so both position and 
orientation affect the responses 
of fMRI recording sites in early 
visual cortex.
6
• By looking at the brain 
activity in primary visual 
cortex, we can determine 
which of a set of images a 
subject was seeing
Determining image content from 
brain activity
6
Kay et al, Nature 2008
By looking at the brain activity in primary 
visual cortex, we can make a model of 
each recording site’s preferred position 
and orientation.
We do this by determining where the 
edges in an image lie, when the image was 
shown, and when the recording site 
responded.
By looking at these factors for a large set 
of images, the model gains information 
about which combinations of orientation 
and position produce a response in each 
recording site. 
Specifically, each orientation and position 
is given a ‘weight’ for each recording site, 
and the weight is learned from the 
responses to training set images, using 
multiple linear regression. (CHECK 
FAMILIARITY WITH THIS CONCEPT).
So here the machine learning approach 

--- Page 3 ---
aims to learn the parameters of a specific 
neural response model that we ALREADY 
KNOW describes the early visual cortex’s 
responses accurately.
From the resulting pattern of weights and 
the locations and orientations found in a 
new image, the researcher can then predict 
the pattern of responses we would expect 
for the new image, in an independent test 
set that has not been shown before.
We can then compare this prediction to the 
observed pattern of responses.
The best fit is most likely to be the image 
the participant was viewing.
Classifiers 
(biological based)
Kay et al, Nature 2008
This approach identifies the 
displayed image far above 
chance (shown by the green line 
at the bottom of the right graph).
Performance is still far above 
chance when the image is only 
shown once, the black lines are 
for single trials.
Note here that the images are 
chosen from a set of possible 
candidates.
It is not possible to reconstruct 
what the image looked like, only 
to compare the pattern of 
responses against some 
predictions of what we would 
expect for a set of candidate 
images.
This is a limitation, but we can 
use an exceptionally large set of 
candidate images, for example 
‘all the images on the internet’.

--- Page 4 ---
8
And by also learning what 
movies look like...
8
Nishimoto et al, Current Biology 2011
Because the performance at candidate image 
identification is still high from single presentations 
of an image, we can use very even do this for movie 
clips.
We can also make the candidate set a very large set 
of movies, for example every two seconds of 
footage from Youtube.
For each of these video clips, we can make a 
predicted pattern of fMRI responses, by looking at 
the positions and orientations of the contrast they 
contain.
We can then see which movie clips best predict the 
observed pattern and average them together to 
predict what the displayed clip is likely to have 
looked like.
Here, we have implicitly given information about 
what movies look like, together with an explicit 
model of how neurons respond.
Therefore, faces generally appear white and the sky 
appears blue, although neither the brain’s fMRI 
responses nor the position-and-orientation 
response model have information about color.
-Remember here that simple machine learning 
could determine what orientation people were 
looking at, and that was exciting and impressive in 
2006.
-Including known biological properties of the 
system quickly allowed researchers to reconstruct 
movies of what people were looking at from the 
activity of the same areas.
Modelling visual image 
responses
• We can model responses to training visual images to determine 
which test image was shown from the pattern of neural activity.
• Model each recordings site’s response as the sum of 
positions and orientations it responds to
• Biologically inspired model: follows known neural response 
properties
• This makes predictions of the neural response we would expect 
to see for any candidate image
• Using a very large set of candidate images, we can effectively 
reconstruct the presented image or movie from brain activity
• But this implicitly biases the reconstruction towards the 
content of the candidate image set
-We can therefore derive some 
good models of simple visual 
processing from responses to a 
training set of natural images, by 
including a biological model 
whose parameters we want to 
estimate. The resulting models 
make accurate predictions about 
responses to new images in the 
test set.

Mathematics and quantities

-This gives us a lot of information 
about simple visual processing, 
but now let’s look at a higher 
cognitive function, quantity 
processing
The world around us contains many 
quantities that are important in 
guiding our behaviour 
DESCRIBE EACH 
In all of these cases, we can use 
numerical symbols to linguistically 
signify the quantity.
Quantity-selective neural responses
Nieder & 
Miller, (2002) 
Science;
(2003) 
Neuron;
(2004) PNAS
Neural response 
amplitude
So, if we want to investigate the 
representation of quantities with 
encoding models, what do we 
know about the brain’s response 
to quantities?
Perhaps the most studied neural 
response to quantities is in 
numerosity-selective neurons. 
These show a response that peaks 
for a specific number of items, the 
preferred numerosity. 
Different neural populations prefer 
different numerosities (peak for 
different numbers of items). 
First found in a large set of brain 
areas in macaque monkeys, these 
have since been described in 
humans and crows. 
In fact, far simpler species seem to 
have some sense of number, 
including bees and newborn 
chickens. So quantity processing is 
not unique to humans

--- Page 6 ---
Number representations with 
SVM classifiers
Presented
Predicted
Eger et al, 2009 Current Biology
Here we see an SVM classifier in the 
parietal cortex can decode which 
number was shown with about 70% 
accuracy
If we also look at the mistakes made, 
this shows us that errors normally 
choose for a nearby number, not a far 
number.
These errors suggest there is some 
relationship between neural activity 
responding to nearby numbers: 
specifically, representational 
dissimilarity increases with numerical 
distance.
So this shows us there is some spatial 
pattern in responses to numbers in 
these areas. But what is this pattern?
Map organisation sensory & 
motor cortices
We had some ideas about the type 
of pattern to expect from other 
systems in the brain 
We have already seen that the 
somatosensory and motor areas of 
the brain map the body’s 
organisation onto the cortical 
surface.
Map organisation sensory 
cortices
We see something similar in the 
brain’s visual cortex.
-Here we see colors representing 
preferred positions in visual space 
overlaid on the occipital lobe’s 
anatomy
-This reveals that visual position 
preferences change gradually across 
the brain, again forming multiple 
maps of visual space on the cortical 
surface

--- Page 7 ---
Map organisation sensory 
cortices
- This doesn’t just work for position though. In the 
auditory cortex, the map reflects auditory 
frequency
- The colors here represent the frequency that 
gives the largest response at every recording site
((- In fact, these maps don’t really represent space 
directly, but rather the position on the sensory 
organ.
- For the human brain, visual field position is 
better understood as a location on the retina, 
while responses to auditory frequency are 
responses for locations on the cochlea, the ear’s 
sensory organ, where different locations vibrate at 
different frequencies.
-Within these maps, neurons with similar 
responses are grouped together, and this leads to 
map organisation))
-But outside of sensory systems like these, we still 
didn’t have a good idea of how the brain 
processes and organises information.
-Often, we only know which large areas are 
activated by a particular stimulus class or task, or 
which large areas contain information about 
these.
((-When we started thinking beyond these 
simplest sensory properties, it seemed quite 
possible that such map structure could only follow 
sensory organ structures, as the neural pathways 
from the sensory organs already follow sensory 
organ structures.))
Biological models of number 
processing
• Neurons with similar properties 
interact
•
Allows useful computations
• Organisational consequences
•
Topographic maps (retinotopy, 
tonotopy, somatotopy)
• Are responses to numerosity also 
mapped onto the brain’s surface?
•
Using fMRI (7T) & model-based 
analyses
After Gray (1918)
This is an important question for 
neuroscience.
We don’t have sensory organs 
with numerical structure, so 
maps organised by numerosity 
preferences would demonstrate 
that the same organisation can 
be found in cognitive processing 
systems.
-Cognitive functions are 
appealing because they are often 
absent or very different in 
animals
-So to understand the neural 

--- Page 8 ---
processing underlying cognition, 
it’s best to measure in humans. 
-But the methods to do that can’t 
look at single neurons because 
we can’t make holes in our 
participant’s heads.
-Maps like these group 
similarly-responding neurons, so 
that the responses of a large 
group of neurons can tell us a lot 
about the individual neurons 
they contain.
-This would allow us to study 
the computational properties of

Neural response amplitude
If we look within any of these areas, we 
see some very different types of 
response
In the upper trace locations, we see 
large responses just after presenting 
small numerosities.
In the lower trace locations, very close 
by, we see large responses following 
large numerosities.
So, we want to model these responses 
to understand the underlying neural 
responses. We know that these areas are 
likely to be tuned for numerosity, with 
response amplitude peaking at a 
particular numerosity and dropping with 
distance from that numerosity.
Therefore, we will make models of tuned 
responses, and learn each recording 
site’s tuning parameters.

We describe this response using a model 
of the population neural response, 
together with the stimulus time course.
The neural response model has two 
parameters: preferred numerosity and 
tuning width
We took this model from the known 
response properties of numerosity-
selective neurons in monkeys.
We examine the overlap of the neural 
model with the stimulus sequence to 
predict the neural response time course 
if this neural model saw this stimulus 
sequence. 
But fMRI doesn’t record neural 
responses directly, it instead records the 
slow changes in blood flow in the 
several seconds following the neural 
activity. So to predict the fMRI response 
time course resulting from this neural 
response time course, we then convolve 
neural response time course with an 
HRF.
BAD MATCH between prediction and 
measurement
CHANGE MODEL RESPONSE SELECTIVITY, 
GOOD MODEL FIT
So again our model is using a 
biologically inspired model and finding 
the best fitting model parameters, which 
we can use to answer our research 
question.

Different fMRI responses explained
This reveals the numerosity 
tuning model parameters that 
predict the timing of the 
responses we see.
Importantly, this model 
summarises the responses to all 
numerosities using just two 
parameters, the preferred 
numerosity (which produces the 
largest response) and the tuning 
width (the range of numerosities 
that produce a large response).
Each of these parameters has a 
straightforward biological 
interpretation, and we can 
analyse the variation of these 
parameters within and between 
brain areas. 
This can tell us far more about 
the system we are studying than 
the unconstrained input-output 
mapping in decoding models, 
but it is still trying to learn the 
relationship between the input 
numerosity and the output 
response pattern.

-In each of these areas, the preferred 
numerosity of recording sites (colors) 
changes gradually and highly 
significantly across the cortical surface
-We repeated this on different days 
using stimuli that control for low-level, 
non-numerical features that sometimes 
change with numerosity, the colored 
lines.
-This is an informal test of model 
generalisation: model parameters are 
similar in on different recording 
sessions with different relationships 
between numerosity and the visual 
image
-So this already shows some 
information about how numbers are 
processed: neurons with similar 
response preferences are grouped 
together to allow easy interactions

Quantity-selective neural responses
And these are closely related brain 
areas, which suggests that the brain 
uses related systems for processing 
different quantities.
What about symbolic numbers?

We can also use the same design 
to look for responses to 
symbolic numbers, written 
numerals.
If we see similar responses, this 
would tell us that written 
numbers are represented 
similarly to the number of 
objects we are seeing.
This is not possible to study in 
animals because they do not 
understand written numbers as 
humans do.
AT END: So this tells us that 
written numbers are not 

--- Page 12 ---
represented the same way as 
numbers of objects.
It seems these neural 
populations are responding to 
the visual impression of numbers 
of items, rather than the 
cognitive concept of number.
Harvey, Ferri & Orban (2017) 
Trends in Cognitive Sciences
 
Castaldi, Vignaud & Eger (2020) 
Neuroimage
Quantities > Number symbols
Math tasks recruit the Intraparietal Sulcus
This is what we see here when we 
compare responses to similar tasks 
done with physical quantities and 
symbolic number. The produce 
responses in quite distinct areas. 
Once we start doing quantitative 
tasks, even simple comparisons, a 
nearby but distinct system gets 
involved. 
This is not just encoding the 
quantities, but actually working with 
them. 
While many animals can make 
decisions based on quantity, all but 
the simplest cognitive tasks using 
numbers seem unique to humans. 
Notably, area responding during 
comparisons and calculations lies 
between areas responding to 
physical quantities and symbolic 
numbers, which may allow it to do 
similar operations with both inputs. 
So this area, deep in the 
intraparietal sulcus, seems to be 
activated when we do these 
complex tasks. 

--- Page 13 ---
But this doesn’t tell us HOW the 
brain processes complex tasks 
using these neural populations. 
Task complexity responses
Van Ackooij, Paul, Hendrikx, van Helden, Gayet, van der Stoep, Harvey (coming soon)
So we designed an experiment to 
investigate how the brain’s 
responses change when we change 
visual task complexity, or the ‘load’ 
on visual short-term memory. 
This is again a step further away 
from sensory processing, we are 
now looking for responses to the 
complexity of a task rather than 
features of sensory stimuli. 
(A) Every 4.5 seconds, we showed 
a display that participants had to 
remember. We then showed an 
item that either matches one of 
the remembered items or is 
different. Participants had to 
respond whether it was the 
same or different. 
(B) In different trials, we changed 
how many items were unique, so 
had to be remembered. When 
only 1 or 2 different orientations 
are shown, this is easy. But with 
6 different orientations it is very 
complex. This allowed us to 
gradually change task 
complexity over time. 

--- Page 14 ---
(C) When we look at responses in 
different fMRI voxels in one 
brain, we see they respond 
maximally at different times, so 
respond most to different 
memory task loads.
Van Ackooij, Paul, 
Hendrikx, van Helden, 
Gayet, van der Stoep, 
Harvey (coming soon)

Task complexity responses
So we see that the same principles 
of tuned responses and changing 
their preferences across the cortical 
surface also applies to task 
complexity. 
In other words, processing of 
complex tasks is distributed 
between neural populations, within 
brain areas, and between brain 
areas.  
This is much like we see for early 
sensory spatial processing and 
physical quantity processing.  
It suggests that these mechanisms 
of processing complex tasks have 
been recycled from sensory 
processing systems to use in task 
processing systems that have 
evolved in humans’ greatly enlarged 
parietal and frontal lobes.
Modelling neural responses 
to quantities
• Gradually changing numerosity or timing, we can model the underlying 
neural response selectivity throughout the human brain
• Very simple, 2-parameter model for numerosity responses
• The model parameters have straightforward biological interpretations
• We can then analyse relationships between these parameters and how 
they change within and between brain areas
• Similar approaches can also be applied to the brain’s responses during 
task processing
• Again, tuned responses to different task loads
• Suggests the brain distributes tasks demands across neural 
populations within and between brain areas
• Gives a way to look at how complex tasks are processed, in humans
• Animals can’t do many complex tasks

--- Page 16 ---
We are very good at reading faces
Now we’ll take a look at how we 
use much more complex ML to 
understand how the brain 
processes object identity. Face 
perception is a common model 
of object processing.
We are so good with faces that 
this 3-second clip gives us a lot 
of information: age, gender, 
complex emotional state etc.
Object-selective responses
But recognition of faces and 
other objects has been one of 
the hardest cognitive processes 
to understand.
Somewhere along the line, 
researchers started finding cells 
that respond to particular classes 
of object, such as faces
Face-selectivity in fMRI
We also see brain areas that 
respond when we show faces, 
but respond less to other object 
classes.
The cells involved are grouped 
together in specific areas of the 
cortex.
Because we can see clear 
differences between response 
amplitudes to faces and other 
objects, it is straightforward to 
make a classifier that can 
distinguish these responses.

--- Page 17 ---
Object selective responses
As we have already seen, there are lot of 
brain areas responding to various object 
types, labelled here with different colors. 
However, it has been very hard to 
understand how such responses arise. 
Some very exciting progress has been 
made here recently, by combining advanced 
machine learning with the known properties 
of the early visual system. 
We understand V1’s role in form processing 
as an oriented edge detector. 
It has even been very hard to get a useful 
description of what differs between the 
properties of V1 and the next processing 
layer, V2.
Object Perception
Why is it so difficult?
36
It has taken many years to understand how 
these object-selective responses arise 
Because the identity of any object has little 
relationship to its impression on the 
retina.
Here we see the result of a google image 
search for pictures of cats.
We can recognise objects from different 
viewpoint and sizes, in different positions, 
and with different lighting conditions
So we can’t recognise an object directly 
from its impression on the eye or camera 
sensor
The 20th century view of 
object recognition
• Stage 1 builds a piecemeal 
representation of local 
image properties.
• Stage 2 builds a 
representation of larger-
scale shapes and surfaces.
• Stage 3 matches shapes 
and surfaces with stored 
object representations-
recognition.

--- Page 18 ---
The 20th century view of 
object recognition
So we might think that our 
object representations are built 
from combinations of feature 
representations
Indeed, objects are built from 
parts, so simplified parts that we 
recognise from all angles might 
let us build a object viewpoint-
independent object 
representation.
Unfortunately, we don’t find 
neurons that look like the middle 
stages of this network.
What do later visual areas do? (2013 version)
Beyond V1, it has even been hard to figure 
out what V2 does, only the second stage in 
cortical visual processing. Generally, it 
responds very similarly to V1 when presented 
with oriented edges.
-Because V1 only responds to the spatial 
scales and orientations of contrast in its 
input, we can make images with the same 
distribution of spatial scale and orientation as 
a natural image, and V1 will respond equally 
well to both.
-However, V2 responds more strongly to 
natural images than these noise patterns. 
-To make similar responses in V2, the image 
needs to have a similar pattern of local 
correlations of orientation as the natural 
image.
-This appears to be because such patterns of 
local correlations are common in natural 
images that have trained the filters linking V1 
to V2 
i.e. it responds when inputs have the 
correlations that are common in the real 
world. By V2, if it fires together, it wires 
together.
-Orientation-selective responses in V1 were 
first discovered in 1959. It took until 2013 to 
reveal how these were transformed by V2. 
Before 2013, researchers tried to explain V2’s 
responses with reference to features in the 
input image, rather than patterns in V1’s 
output.
-Beyond V2, we still don’t have a good 
feeling for what drives responses, but we now 
understand it is likely to be a feature 
transformation from the outputs of previous 
network layers.

--- Page 19 ---
Deep convolutional neural 
networks
DCNNs try to imitate this 
structure over many levels
The neurons in each visual area 
look very similar in their 
connections to other neurons.
So DCNNs suppose that, after an 
initial edge detection, each layer 
performs similar analyses of the 
activity in the previous network 
layer.
Specifically, each layer learns 
patterns of responses across a 
small area’s representation in 
the previous layer
So DCNNs work only where there 
are meaningful relationships 
between neighbouring inputs, 
like neighbouring spatial 
locations or neighbouring times
Even after two layers, it becomes 
difficult for humans to 
understand what a ‘neuron’ (or 
unit) will respond to.
In the case of DCNNs, the 
network follows biological 
knowledge, but is very complex 
with lots of weights to learn.
The learning therefore becomes 
very computationally intensive, 
but can do impressively human-
like things.
A proper understanding of how 
deep networks work and the 
biological systems they imitate is 
beyond this class, but is covered 
in Machine Learning for Human 
Vision and Language, my class in 
period 1.

--- Page 20 ---
Emergence of object 
selective responses
Here we see a unit in the top layer 
of this DCNN after supervised 
learning of a large set of natural 
images 
The network finds patterns and 
statistical structure in this natural 
image training set
The unit here (red line) responds to 
faces regardless of their position, 
size, orientation.
The pattern of responses closely 
follows how face-response neurons 
in monkeys respond (black line)
Inverting an object 
recognition DCNN
But it is important to understand that deep 
networks have increasingly complex 
representations of objects in the images, over 
several network layers
To get a feel for what features are 
represented in different at different levels, we 
can modify the input image to find a version 
that produces the strongest possible 
response at each layer.
After training to classify the objects in natural 
images, successive layers respond best to 
increasingly complex relationships between 
pixels in the input image. These relationships 
follow the local correlations and patterns 
found in natural images
Inverting an object 
recognition DCNN
This network has been trained on images 
from the internet, which contains many 
pictures of animals
Therefore the higher level units have a lot of 
animal representations.
So these ‘inverted’ networks impose the 
response preferences of higher-level layers 
on the network’s representation of the input 
image.
Much of how we see the world also follows 
our previous experience, so we don’t have to 
process each image so extensively: the brain 
also constrains object recognition problems 
using established neural network weights.
We currently believe that hallucinogenic 
drugs act by amplifying these feedback 
signals, biasing our perception towards 
previous experience.
Many psychedelic drug users comment that 
these images look much like hallucinations 
they have experienced

Probabilistic models, lecture 1
Cognitive modelling

--- Page 2 ---
2
Probabilistic models
• Cognitive models based on the theory of probability.
• One popular class – Bayesian models, which are used to
describe learning and reasoning.
• Bayesian models can go beyond learning and reasoning and
can capture memory and categorization, among others.
(Anderson, 1991)
• Bayesian models are part of Rational theory of cognition.

--- Page 3 ---
3
Application of Bayesian models
Language
• L01: probabilistic models of learning
• L02: probabilistic models of reasoning

--- Page 4 ---
4
This class
• How is syntax acquired?
We will focus on rule-based learning and a symbolic system
(the Language of Thought)
• How can logic, linguistics and a probability theory be
combined to address the question of learning of syntax?

--- Page 14 ---
8
Language of thought
Language of Thought (Fodor, 1975)
• Humans have access to basic primitives and basic logical
operations (union, intersection, composition etc.).
• They compose primitives into more complex concepts using
these basic logical operations.
• They learn complex concepts by applying Bayes rule.

--- Page 15 ---
9
Rational analysis
• If behavior is optimized to the environment, then cognitive
processes must implement this optimal relation.
• In particular, learning should be optimal (rational).

--- Page 16 ---
10
Plan for today
1 A quick recap on formal languages
2 A quick recap on probabilities
3 Modeling syntax learning from Yang and Piantadosi, 2022
4 Going beyond syntax learning, looking at number learning and
concept learning.

2 How one can think of Bayesian models as cognitive models.
3 How we can reverse-engineer our understanding about
underlying cognitive structures.
4 What is challenging in learning syntax and how learning can
be cross-domain.

This lecture
Bayesian update is used to model reasoning.
Bayes’ rule: P(A|B) = P(B|A)·P(A)
P(B)
• Bayes’ rule tells us how people embed new findings into their
beliefs about some hypothesis (A).

This lecture
Bayesian update is used to model reasoning.
• We will use Bayesian models to capture reasoning in
communication.
• When we talk: we often reason about what’s been said, why,
and also, what has not been said.
• This reasoning moves us beyond the plain message
(conventional meaning).

--- Page 10 ---
Newell’s time scales (of action)
• NOT:
– How long does the task take in total?
• Instead:
– At what level is the task MODELLED? 
– What are the smallest units of action in the model
PRP effect

driving

Societal 
implications

of action
ms
s
min

--- Page 11 ---
Marr’s levels
• NOT:
– Was an algorithm used? In the context of computer 
simulations, algorithms are (almost) always used
• Instead: does the model focus on:
– Computational problem (why certain behavior?) 
Typically: optimization accounts (e.g., probabilistic; 
Bayes; some forms of ML)
– Algorithmic problem (which steps are achieved?)
Typically: process models
– Implementation problem (How is this implemented 
in the brain?)
Typically: neural models (e.g. ML)

FILE: Poster\Cognitive Modeling Poster.pptx

--- Slide 1 ---


Using Deep Learning to Predict Human Decisions and Using Cognitive Models to Explain Deep Learning Models
Authors: Matan Fintz, Margarita Osadchy & Uri Hertz
Presenters:  Alimohamed Jaffer, Lui Holohan, Yangfan Chen,  Ruben de Koning
Introduction
Methods
Discussion
Cognitive Modeling using Machine Learning Approach: high flexibility but low interpretability compared to theory-driven models.
Author's Approach: use both theory-driven and deep learning models. We utilize high-capacity deep-network models to capture complex cognitive processes in the general population, and then use explicit theory-driven models to explain this cognitive process to characterize the black-box deep learning model.
General setup: uses deep neural networks (DNNs) as exploratory tools to predict human decisions, in a four-armed bandit task. And then pair DNN predictions with reward-oriented model and reward-oblivious model
Exploratory Model: LSTM Deep Neural Network:
Used to predict human decision-making for four-armed bandit task
Implementation:
LSTM architecture; used as exploratory model
Single layer LSTM with 64 hidden units
Trained on 4-step sequences of actions and rewards
300 epochs, batch size 2048
Strengths: highly accurate in predicting participant action (72.3%), provides new insights into cognitive processes, high capacity, data driven.
Limitations: lack of explainability (black box model), not useful in that sense as scientific tool - explanation is more important

Theory-driven Reward-Oriented Model:
Implements Q-learning algorithm
Chooses the most rewarding option
Assumes participants make decisions based on learned value associated with each option
Implementation:
Updates Q-values based on prediction errors
Q(t+1) = Q(t) + α[r(t) - Q(t)]
Uses softmax distribution for choices
Limitations: simplifying assumption (reward based) - doesn't model exploration, lower accuracy than DNN (68.2%). 

Theory-driven Reward-Oblivious Model:
Key Insight: During periods of uncertainty (not clear which selection results in reward-maximization), reward-based assumption does not explain human behaviour. 
May be based on specific patterns of exploration, e.g., repeat sequences based on previous outcomes, explore one option at a time.
Can coincide with reward-oriented behaviour, e.g., choosing same action again and again when there is a clear best choice
Implementation:
Model is trained on chain-of-actions
Same data as DNN model, without rewards
Model is also LSTM, to mitigate extraneous variables and focus on lack of rewards (action outcome) as only difference
Architecture and training procedure the same
Uses 4-step action sequences of human participants
Performance: 69.9% accuracy on human behavior, >94% on pattern completion
Accurate predictions with exploratory DNNs: It achieved an accuracy of 72.3%. Outperforming reward-oriented (68.2%) and reward-oblivious (69.9%) models.
Convergence with theory-driven models: The DNN aligned with the reward-oriented model under some conditions and with the reward-oblivious model under others.
High-capacity exploratory models as tools for scientific investigation: By fitting data to these models, we detect patterns and regularities beyond theory-driven approaches, revealing how much of the data can be explained and predicted.
Interpreting DNNs with cognitive models: Our approach combines DNNs, which uncover behavioral patterns often dismissed as noise, with explicit cognitive models to generate interpretable predictions, distinguishing reward-oriented and reward-oblivious processes.
